Lecture details

L01-1-1

In Grimson's 2008 introduction to Python, what stopping test and update rule does the square-root recipe use?

  • Stop and return the guess g when g squared is sufficiently close to x.
  • Otherwise replace g with (g + x/g)/2 and repeat.
Transcript evidence

L01-1-2

In the 2008 Python introduction, how does Grimson distinguish the string '52' from the number 52?

  • Quotes mark a sequence of characters as a string; 52 without quotes is a number.
  • Their types determine which operations apply and what those operations mean.
Transcript evidence

Or if you want to think of it as a real number. And there's some others that we can see. L01-1-3

In Grimson's stored-program computer explanation, what does the program counter point to, and how can a test change execution?

  • It points to the location of an instruction in memory.
  • Execution normally advances to the next instruction; a test can change the counter to another instruction location.
Transcript evidence

6.00 — Lecture 1: Computer Science and Python Introduction · 25:02–29:02 · 1:machine

recipe, the description of a sequence of steps, take that as its input, and then that machine will now act like what is described in that recipe. Reconfigure itself, emulate it, however you want to use the words, it's going to change how it does the computation. That would be cool. And that exists. It's called an interpreter. It is the basic heart of every computer. What it is doing, is saying, change the game. This is now an example of a stored-program computer. What that means, in a stored-program computer, is that I can provide to the computer a sequence of instructions describing the process I want it to execute. And inside of the machine, and things we'll talk about, there is a process that will allow that sequence to be executed as described in that recipe, so it can behave like any thing that I can describe in one of those recipes. All right. That actually seems like a really nice thing to have, and so let me show you what that would basically look like. Inside of a stored-program computer, we would have the following: we have a memory, it's connected to two things; control unit, in what's called an ALU, an arithmetic logic unit, and this can take in input, and spit out output, and inside this stored-program computer, excuse me, you have the following: you have a sequence of instructions. And these all get stored in there. Notice the difference. The recipe, the sequence of instructions, is actually getting read in, and it's treated just like data. It's inside the memory of the machine, which means we have access to it, we can change it, we can use it to build new pieces of code, as well as we can interpret it. One other piece that goes into this computer-- I never remember where to put the PC, John, control? ALU? Separate? I'll put it separate-- you have a thing called a program counter. And here's the basis of the computation. That program counter points to some location in memory, typically to the first instruction in the sequence. And those instructions, by the way, are very simple: they're things like, take the value out of two places in memory, and run them through the multiplier in here, a little piece of circuitry, and stick them back into someplace in memory. Or take this value out of memory, run it through some other simple operation, stick it back in memory. Having executed this instruction, that counter goes up by one and we move to the next one. We execute that instruction, we move to the next one. Oh yeah, it looks a whole lot like that. Some of those instructions will involve tests: they'll say, is something true? And if the test is true, it will change the value of this program counter to point to some other place in the memory, some other point in that sequence of instructions, and you'll keep processing. Eventually you'll hopefully stop, and a value gets spit out, and you're done. That's the heart of a computer. Now that's a slight misstatement. The process to control it is intriguing and interesting, but the heart of the computer is simply this notion that we build our descriptions, our recipes, on a sequence of primitive instructions. And then we have a flow of control. And that flow of control is what I just described. It's moving through a sequence of instructions, occasionally changing where we are as we move around. OK. Understanding

L01-2-1

Why does a definition of a square root not, by itself, provide a procedure for computing one? Explain using declarative and imperative knowledge.

  • Declarative knowledge states what is true and can let us check a proposed answer.
  • Imperative knowledge specifies executable steps, tests and a stopping condition for finding the answer.
Transcript evidence

L01-2-2

Why can a program pass a syntax check and still give the wrong answer? Use Grimson's distinction between syntax and semantics.

  • Legal expression structure is different from meaningful operations and intended behavior.
  • A program can run with valid syntax and still perform the wrong computation; syntax checking does not establish correctness.
Transcript evidence

6.00 — Lecture 1: Computer Science and Python Introduction · 39:02–44:00 · 1:semantics

So what's syntax? Syntax basically says, what are the legal expressions in this language? Boy, my handwriting is atrocious, isn't it? There's a English sequence of words. It's not since syntactically correct, right? It's not a sentence. There's no verb in there anywhere, it's just a sequence of nouns. Same thing in our languages. We have to describe how do you put together legally formed expressions. OK? And as we add constructs to the language, we're going to talk about. Second thing we want to talk about very briefly as we go along is the semantics of the language. And here we're going to break out two pieces; static semantics and full semantics. Static semantics basically says which programs are meaningful. Which expressions make sense. Here's an English sentence. It's syntactically correct. Right? Noun phrase, verb, noun phrase. I'm not certain it's meaningful, unless you are in the habit of giving your furniture personal names. What's the point? Again, you can have things that are syntactically legal but not semantically meaningful, and static semantics is going to be a way of helping us decide what expressions, what pieces of code, actually have real meaning to it. All right? The last piece of it is, in addition to having static semantics, we have sort of full semantics. Which is, what does the program mean? Or, said a different way, what's going to happen when I run it? That's the meaning of the expression. That's what you want. All right? You want to know, what's the meaning of this piece of code? L01-2-3

Why does Python allow '3' * 3 but reject '3' * '3', according to the operator discussion in the introductory lecture?

  • String times integer means repetition, producing the string 333 in the first case.
  • Two strings do not have a supported multiplication operation; operand types control the meaning and validity of the operator.
Transcript evidence

Application

L01-3-1

Use the lecture's square-root recipe with x = 9 and g = 2. Stop only when |g*g - x| < 0.01. Should it stop now, and what is the next guess?

  • The current squared error is |4 - 9| = 5, so the stopping test fails.
  • The next guess is (2 + 9/2)/2 = 3.25.

L01-3-2

Consider these expressions separately: '52' + '7', 52 + 7, and '52' * '7'. What does each produce or why does it fail?

  • The first concatenates strings to give '527'; the second adds numbers to give 59.
  • The third fails because multiplication is not defined between two strings.
Transcript evidence

L01-3-3

Use the Python 2-style integer division demonstrated in the 2008 lecture, with no future-division import: what are 7 / 2 and 7 / 2.0, and why do they differ?

  • 7 / 2 gives the integer 3 under the specified historical division behavior.
  • 7 / 2.0 gives 3.5 because a floating-point operand requests real division.

The version is intentional. Do not apply Python 3 true division to the first expression; naming that distinction is welcome but not required.

Connections

L01-4-1

Connect Grimson's idea of an executable recipe with Winston's farmer–fox–goose–grain state graph. Why does representing the legal states still leave an algorithmic task to perform?

  • The state graph describes situations, allowed transitions and constraints.
  • An algorithm must still specify how to explore transitions, choose the next step and recognize the goal; that is the imperative recipe.

L01-4-2

How does the queue-based depth-first search procedure illustrate the sequence, tests and changing control flow described in the introductory programming lecture?

  • Search initializes a queue, tests a path, expands it when needed and repeats.
  • The goal/dead-end tests and queue updates determine what happens next, illustrating controlled execution rather than a bare statement of the desired answer.

L01-4-3

Relate the Python lecture's emphasis on data representation to the nearest-neighbor lecture's magazine example. Why can a valid numeric representation still lead to an unhelpful comparison?

  • A representation determines the operations available but does not automatically make those operations appropriate for the task.
  • Raw word-count vectors are numeric, yet Euclidean distance can favor short documents; comparing vector direction addresses the length mismatch.

This is a synthesis of the two examples, not a claim that the Python lecture discusses document retrieval.

How do you go about finding the articles that are relevant to your question? So this is a puzzle that has been studied for decades by people interested in information retrieval. And here's the simple way to do it. I'm going to illustrate, once again, in just two dimensions. But it has to be applied in many, many dimensions. The idea is you count up the words in the articles in your library, and you compare the word counts to the word counts in your probing question. So you might be interested in 100 words. I'm only going to write two on the board for illustration. So we're going to think about articles from two magazines. Well first of all, what words are we going to use? One word is going to be hack, and that will include all derivatives of hack-- hacker, hacking, and so on. And the other word is going to be computer. And so it would not be surprising for you to see that articles from Wired Magazine might appear in places like this. They would involve lots of uses of the word computer, and lots of uses of the word hack. And now for the sake of illustration, the second magazine from which we are going to draw articles is Town and Country. It's a very tony magazine, and the people who read out Town and Country tend to be social parasites. And they still use the word hack. Because you can talk about hacking, there's some sort of specialize term of art in dealing with horses. So all the Town and Country articles would be likely to be down here somewhere. And maybe they would be one like that when they talk about hiring some computer expert to keep track of the results so the weekly hunt, or something. And now, in you come with your probe. And of course your probe question is going to be relatively small. It's not going to have a lot of words in it. So here's your here's your probe question. Here's your unknown. Which article's going to be closest? Which articles are going to be closest? Well, alas, all those Town and Country articles are closest. So you can't use the nearest neighbor idea, it would seem. Anybody got a suggestion for how we might get out of this dilemma? Yes, Christopher. CHRISTOPHER: If you're looking for word counts and you want to include some terms of computer, then wouldn't you want to use that as a threshold, rather than the nearest neighbor? PROF. PATRICK WINSTON: I don't know, it's a good idea. It might work, who knows. Doug? So the angle between the vectors, well let's actually measure the cosine of the angle between the vectors. Let's see how we can calculate that. So we'll take the cosine of the angle between the vectors, we'll call it theta. That's going to be equal to the sum of the unknown values times the article values. Those are just the values in various dimensions. And then we'll divide that by the magnitude of the other vectors. So that's just the dot product of those two vectors. And it works like a charm. It's not the most sophisticated way of doing these things. There are hairy ways. You can get a Ph.D. by doing this sort of stuff in some new and sophisticated way. But this is a simple way. It works pretty well.

6.034 — Lecture 1: Introduction and Scope

Patrick Henry Winston · MIT OpenCourseWare · Video · Course source · CC BY-NC-SA 4.0

Lecture details

L02-1-1

In Winston's Introduction and Scope lecture, which three activities does his broad definition of artificial intelligence target?

  • Thinking, perception and action.
  • He frames AI in terms of models of these activities, supported by representations, constraints and algorithms.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 1: Introduction and Scope · 02:00–04:00 · 2:scope

I'll explain why we have these covenants at the end. So what is it? Well, it must have something to do with thinking. So let's start up here, a definition of artificial intelligence, by saying that it's about thinking, whatever that is. My definition of artificial intelligence has to be rather broad. So we're going to say it's not only about thinking. It's also about perception, and it's about action. And if this were a philosophy class, then I'd stop right there and just say, in this subject we're going to talk about problems involving thinking, perception, and action. But this is not a philosophy class. This a Course six class. It's an engineering school class. It's an MIT class. So we need more than that. And therefore we're going to talk about models that are targeted at thinking, perception, and action. And this should not be strange to you, because model making is what MIT is about. You run into someone at a bar, or relative asks you what you do at MIT, the right knee jerk reaction is to say, we learned how to build models. That's what we do at MIT. We build the models using differential equations. We build models using probabilities. We build models using physical and computational simulations. Whatever we do, we build models. Even in humanities class, MIT approach is to make models that we can use to explain the past, predict the future,

L02-1-2

Which everyday identification example does Winston use to introduce generate-and-test, and what plays the role of the generator?

  • He identifies a tree leaf using a field guide to North American trees.
  • Turning through possible tree descriptions generates candidates; comparing them with the leaf tests the candidates.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 1: Introduction and Scope · 11:18–14:02 · 2:generator

whatever you like. These are the stuff of what artificial intelligence is about-- methods, algorithms, representations. I'd like to give you one more example. It's something we call, in artificial intelligence, generated test. And it's such a simple idea, you'll never hear it again in this subject. But it's an idea you need to add to your repertoire of problem solving methods, techniques, procedures, and algorithms. So here's how it works. Maybe I can explain to best by starting off with an example. Here's a tree leaf I picked off a tree on the way over to class. I hope it's not the last of the species. What is it, what kind of tree? I don't know. I never did learn my trees, or my colors, or my multiplication tables. So I have to go back to this book, the Audubon Society Field Guide to North American Trees. And how would I solve the problem? It's pretty simple. I just turn the pages one at a time, until I find something that looks like this leaf. And then I discover it's a sycamore, or something. MIT's full of them. So when I do that, I do something very intuitive, very natural, something you do all the time. But we're going to give it a name. We're going to call it generate and test. And generate and test method consists of generating some possible solutions, feeding them into a box that tests them, and then out the other side comes mostly failures. But every once in a while we get something that succeeds and pleases us. That's what I did with the leaf. But now you have a name for it. Once you have a name for something, you get power over it. You can start to talk about it. So I can say, if you're doing a generate and test approach to a problem, you better build a generator with certain properties that make generators good. For example, they should not be redundant. They shouldn't give you the same solution twice. They should be informable. They should be able to absorb information such as, this is a deciduous tree. Don't bother looking at the conifers. So once you have a name for something, you can start talking about. And that vocabulary gives you power.
L02-1-3

For the farmer, fox, goose and grain example, how many bank assignments exist before constraints are applied, and why are some missing from the legal-state graph?

  • Four entities with two possible banks give 2^4 = 16 assignments.
  • Unsafe assignments in which something gets eaten are excluded.

Understanding

L02-2-1

What does Winston mean by the Rumpelstiltskin Principle, and why does giving generate-and-test a name help?

  • Naming an idea makes it possible to refer to it, discuss it and attach knowledge to it.
  • Once the method has a name, we can discuss useful properties such as avoiding duplicate candidates and using information to narrow generation.

So many MIT people miss opportunities, because they have a tendency to think that ideas aren't important unless they're complicated. But the most simple ideas in artificial intelligence are often the most powerful. We could teach an artificial intelligence course to you that would be so full of mathematics it would make a Course 18 professor gag. L02-2-2

Why is a state graph a more useful representation of the river-crossing puzzle than a picture of the farmer alone?

  • It explicitly records participant locations and possible moves.
  • It exposes safety constraints and routes between the initial and goal situations, enabling a search procedure.

Why does Winston describe thinking and perception as a loop rather than completely separate abilities in the imagination demonstration?

  • A description triggers an imagined scene or relevant visual memory.
  • Visual processing of that scene provides information used to answer a question, so thinking invokes perception and uses its result.

Application

L02-3-1

A river puzzle has a farmer and four independently positioned possessions, each on either bank. Before checking any safety or boat rules, how many location assignments must a complete enumeration consider? Explain the count.

  • There are five independently binary positions, including the farmer.
  • The count is 2^5 = 32; legality filtering can reduce this set afterward.

A tree-identification program repeatedly tests the same candidate and keeps proposing conifers after being told the leaf is deciduous. What two generator improvements follow from Winston's discussion?

  • Make generation nonredundant, avoiding repeated candidate solutions.
  • Make it informable: use the deciduous-tree information to exclude incompatible candidates.

L02-3-3

Suppose a system has a visual memory of Alice giving a ball to Bob and is asked who has the ball afterward. Describe how Winston's imagine-then-inspect approach could answer, without claiming the system must have memorized that exact question.

  • Use the description to retrieve or construct the relevant transfer scene.
  • Inspect the scene to determine the recipient/possession relation and answer Bob; the same scene can support more than one question.

The names and question are new; the mechanism is adapted from the lecture demonstration.

L02-4-1

Winston says representations expose constraints that enable algorithms. How does the support-vector-machine lecture put that idea into practice with a separating street?

  • Vectors and a separating boundary turn the classification task into a geometric representation.
  • Constraints on positive/negative examples and a margin objective make it possible to optimize a decision rule.

L03-1-1

In the queue implementation of search, where are new paths inserted for depth-first search, and what one change produces breadth-first search?

  • Depth-first search places newly extended paths at the front.
  • Breadth-first search places them at the back of the queue.
Transcript evidence

L03-1-2

In Winston's beam-search demonstration, what beam width is used and what does that width limit?

  • The example uses width two.
  • It keeps the two best paths at a level, judged by closeness to the goal, rather than keeping two successors for every parent independently.

L03-1-3

What three terrain problems does Winston describe for continuous hill climbing near the end of the basic-search lecture?

  • A local maximum and a flat plateau can prevent useful progress.
  • A narrow diagonal ridge can make all sampled cardinal directions look downhill even though an uphill direction exists.
Transcript evidence

space with Hill Climbing? Well, how would you do Hill Climbing in a continuous space? Let's say we're in the mountains, and a big fog has come up. Understanding

L03-2-1

Why does an extended list save work when different search paths reach the same node?

  • It records nodes whose outgoing continuations have already been explored.
  • It avoids repeatedly extending equivalent continuations reached by different paths.

This question concerns the basic search mechanism, not a blanket optimality guarantee for arbitrary weighted search.

Transcript evidence

What we're going to do is we're going to amend our algorithm just a little bit. And we're not going to extend the first path on the queue unless final node never before extended. What we're going to do is we're going to look to see if there-- we've got this path. And we're going to extend it. And it's got a final note. If we've ever extended a path that goes to that final node, and it was a final node on that path, then we're not going to do it again. We got to keep a list of places that have already been the last piece of a path that was extended. Everybody got that? It's a little awkward to say it, because it's the last node we care about. If a path terminates in a node, and if some other path previously terminated in that node and got extended-- we're not going to do it again. Because it's a waste of time. Now, let's see if this actually helps. Now, use the extended list. Let's see, well, gee, we got that place
L03-2-2

In this lecture's hill-climbing search, why does preferring a successor closer to the goal not by itself establish that the complete route is shortest?

  • The heuristic ranks apparent distance remaining rather than all accumulated route costs.
  • A locally attractive direction need not give the best complete route; this ordering is a guide, not an optimality proof.
Transcript evidence

L03-2-3

Why does a width-two beam differ from simply allowing two children from every expanded parent?

  • Beam width constrains the retained frontier across a whole level.
  • Choosing two per parent can create many more than two surviving paths and fails to impose the same global limit.
Transcript evidence

Application

L03-3-1

The queue is [S-A, S-B]. Expanding its first path S-A produces S-A-C followed by S-A-D. There are no loops or goal nodes. What is the resulting queue for depth-first search and for breadth-first search?

  • DFS: [S-A-C, S-A-D, S-B].
  • BFS: [S-B, S-A-C, S-A-D].

L03-3-2

In the lecture's front-sorted hill-climbing queue, expanding S-A generates S-A-C with heuristic 7 and S-A-D with heuristic 2; S-B is already waiting. Smaller estimates are better. What queue order results, and which path is considered next?

  • Sort the new extensions by their heuristic and place them in front: [S-A-D, S-A-C, S-B].
  • S-A-D is next; the lecture sorts the new extensions, not the entire waiting queue.

L03-3-3

At one level, a beam search has four candidate paths ending at A, B, C and D with estimated remaining distances 8, 3, 5 and 1. With beam width two and smaller estimates preferred, which survive? Can a discarded path still belong to the actual best solution?

  • D and B survive because they have the two lowest estimates.
  • Yes: heuristic ranking can discard a path that would ultimately be best; the width limit alone does not guarantee optimality.
Transcript evidence

Connections

L03-4-1

Contrast breadth-first search's level ordering with branch-and-bound's accumulated-distance ordering. Why can fewest edges and lowest route cost be different goals?

  • Breadth-first explores by depth/number of edges, whereas branch-and-bound orders paths by accumulated cost.
  • When edge costs differ, a route with more edges can have lower total cost; the two rankings need not agree.
Transcript evidence

L03-4-2

How does the neural-net lecture adapt the continuous hill-climbing idea, and what extra information does it use instead of just trying a few directions?

  • Both seek improvements in an objective over a parameter space.
  • Neural-net training uses partial derivatives/the gradient to guide weight changes, with a rate constant controlling step size; local-search difficulties are not automatically eliminated.

L03-4-3

How does the extended-list idea implement the nonredundancy property recommended for generate-and-test?

  • Generate-and-test should avoid generating or considering the same solution repeatedly.
  • An extended list prevents repeated expansion of a node reached through multiple paths, eliminating repeated continuation work.
Transcript evidence

whatever you like. These are the stuff of what artificial intelligence is about-- methods, algorithms, representations. I'd like to give you one more example. It's something we call, in artificial intelligence, generated test. And it's such a simple idea, you'll never hear it again in this subject. But it's an idea you need to add to your repertoire of problem solving methods, techniques, procedures, and algorithms. So here's how it works. Maybe I can explain to best by starting off with an example. Here's a tree leaf I picked off a tree on the way over to class. I hope it's not the last of the species. What is it, what kind of tree? I don't know. I never did learn my trees, or my colors, or my multiplication tables. So I have to go back to this book, the Audubon Society Field Guide to North American Trees. MIT's full of them. So when I do that, I do something very intuitive, very natural, something you do all the time. But we're going to give it a name. We're going to call it generate and test. And generate and test method consists of generating some possible solutions, feeding them into a box that tests them, and then out the other side comes mostly failures. But every once in a while we get something that succeeds and pleases us. That's what I did with the leaf. But now you have a name for it. Once you have a name for something, you get power over it. You can start to talk about it. So I can say, if you're doing a generate and test approach to a problem, you better build a generator with certain properties that make generators good. For example, they should not be redundant. They shouldn't give you the same solution twice. They should be informable. They should be able to absorb information such as, this is a deciduous tree. Don't bother looking at the conifers. So once you have a name for something, you can start talking about. And that vocabulary gives you power.

L04-1-3

What stronger heuristic property does Winston introduce after showing that an admissible heuristic can fail with his no-reopening extended-list implementation?

  • Consistency.
  • In the undirected formulation shown, the absolute difference between two heuristic estimates cannot exceed the actual distance between the nodes.

Understanding

L04-2-1

For branch-and-bound with nonnegative edge costs, why can it stop when the least-cost path removed from its queue already reaches the goal, but not necessarily when a goal path is first generated?

  • When the goal path is least-cost, all queued alternatives already cost at least as much and cannot improve through nonnegative extensions.
  • A newly generated goal path can coexist with cheaper unfinished paths, so discovery alone is not the same stopping condition.

L04-2-2

Why does adding an admissible estimate of distance remaining to accumulated route cost help guide an optimal search?

  • Accumulated cost is already spent; the admissible remaining estimate does not overstate the best possible cost still needed.
  • Their sum supplies a lower-bound estimate of a complete route, helping prioritize promising paths and reject paths that cannot beat an incumbent.

For graph search that permanently closes nodes, consistency or an appropriate reopening policy also matters; do not claim admissibility alone makes every implementation correct.

Transcript evidence

L04-2-3

Why can an admissible but inconsistent heuristic cause the lecture's extended-list A* implementation to miss a shorter route?

  • It can cause a node to be expanded through a worse route before a better route is found.
  • The extended list then refuses the later improvement; the lecture strengthens the heuristic condition to consistency.

Keep the implementation qualification: this is not a claim that all forms of A* fail with admissible inconsistent heuristics.

Application

L04-3-1

Three queued routes have (cost so far g, estimated remainder h) values A=(4,6), B=(7,1), C=(3,8). Which is chosen using g+h, and which using g alone?

  • g+h scores are A=10, B=8, C=11, so the heuristic-guided choice is B.
  • Using g alone chooses C, whose accumulated cost is 3.

L04-3-2

A complete route costs 11. Two unfinished routes have accumulated costs 12 and 11, and every remaining edge has nonnegative cost. If we need any shortest route, can both be pruned? Could the second still tie the incumbent?

  • Neither route can improve on 11, so both may be pruned when any one shortest route is sufficient.
  • The cost-11 route could tie if its remaining edges total zero; pruning it does not enumerate every shortest route.

L04-3-3

For an undirected edge X-Y of cost 2, let true remaining distances be 5 from X and 3 from Y, and estimates be h(X)=4 and h(Y)=0. Are these estimates admissible? Do they satisfy the lecture's consistency test on this edge?

  • Both are admissible: 4 <= 5 and 0 <= 3.
  • They are not consistent on this edge because |4-0|=4 exceeds 2.

Connections

L04-4-1

Beam search and A* both use heuristic information. Why does their use of that information give different protection against throwing away the best route?

  • Beam search retains only a fixed number of apparently promising paths and can discard a winning route.
  • Properly implemented A* uses cost plus a suitable lower-bound heuristic rather than a fixed-width cutoff; its optimality relies on the relevant heuristic/graph-search conditions.
Transcript evidence

L04-4-2

What does the dead horse principle have in common in branch-and-bound and alpha-beta pruning, and what is the bound about in each case?

  • Both avoid work on a branch that cannot improve the decision already available.
  • Branch-and-bound compares route-cost bounds with a goal cost; alpha-beta compares game-value bounds under alternating maximizing and minimizing choices.
Transcript evidence

L04-4-3

Compare the role of distance in heuristic route search with its role in nearest-neighbor classification. Why is an airline-distance estimate not interchangeable with a feature-vector similarity score?

  • Route search estimates remaining travel cost to a goal, with lower-bound conditions relevant to optimality.
  • Nearest-neighbor distance or similarity compares feature representations to choose a matching example; it need not be a bound on the cost of a path.

You can get a Ph.D. by doing this sort of stuff in some new and sophisticated way. But this is a simple way. It works pretty well.

In the games lecture, what does a static board evaluator inspect, and which two chess features are mentioned as examples?

  • It evaluates the current board without exploring its future consequences.
  • King safety and pawn structure are named; features can be combined with weights in a linear scoring polynomial.

Now, normally, this function, g, is reduced to a linear polynomial. So, in the end, the most popular kind of way of forming a static value is to take f1, multiply it times some constant, c1, add c2, multiply it times f2. And that is a linear scoring polynomial. So, we could use that function to produce numbers from each of these things and then pick the highest number. And that would be a way of playing the game. Actually, a scoring polynomial is a little bit more than we need. Because all we really need is a method that looks at those three boards and says, I like this one best. It doesn't have to rank them. It doesn't have to give them numbers. All it has to do is say which one it likes best. So, one way of doing that is to use a linear scoring polynomial. But it's not the only way of doing that. L05-1-3

What does the games lecture call the strategy of completing shallower searches before deeper ones so a move is available when time expires?

  • Progressive deepening; iterative deepening is an acceptable equivalent term.
  • It is presented as an anytime approach with a completed shallower result as an insurance policy.

Understanding

L05-2-1

Why does minimax not simply choose the largest leaf value anywhere in the game tree?

  • The opponent controls alternate decisions and seeks to minimize the maximizing player’s value.
  • Values must be backed up using the correct min/max choice at each level; the maximizing player cannot force every attractive leaf.
Transcript evidence

L05-2-2

Does alpha-beta pruning approximate minimax or change its backed-up value? Explain what work it can avoid.

  • For the same tree and leaf evaluations, it preserves the minimax value rather than approximating it.
  • It avoids evaluations and even move generation in subtrees whose bounds show they cannot change the decision.

Tied best moves can be selected differently; do not require an identical tie-breaking choice.

L05-2-3

Why is progressive deepening useful when a game-playing program has a fixed time limit and the branching factor varies?

  • It is hard to know in advance which depth can be completed within the available time.
  • A completed shallower result remains available if the next deeper search cannot finish; earlier levels are relatively inexpensive compared with the deepest level.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 6: Search: Games, Minimax, and Alpha-Beta · 32:00–35:00 · 5:work

And it's not the smallest number. It's the compromised number that's arrived at virtue of the fact that this is an adversarial situation. So, you say to me, how much energy, how much work do you actually saved by doing this? Well, it is the case that in the optimal situation, if everything is ordered right, if God has come down and arranged your tree in just the right way, then the approximate amount of work you need to do, the approximate number of static evaluations performed, is approximately equal to 2 times b to the d over 2. We don't care about this 2. We care a whole lot about that 2. That's the amount of work that's done. It's b to the d over 2, instead of b to d. What's that mean? Suppose that without this idea, I can go down seven levels. How far can I go down with this idea? 14 levels. So, it's the difference between a jerk and a world champion. So, that, however, is only in the optimal case when God has arranged things just right. But in practical situations, practical game situations, it appears to be the case, experimentally, that the actual number is close to this approximation for optimal arrangements. So, you'd never not want to use alpha-beta. It saves an amazing amount of time. You could look at it another way. Suppose you go down the same number of levels, how much less work do you have to do? Well, quite a bit. The square root [INAUDIBLE], right? That's another way of looking at how it works. So, we could go home at this point except for one problem, and that is that we pretended that the branching factor is always the same. But, in fact, the branching factor will vary with the game state and will vary with the game. So, you can calculate how much computing you can do in two minutes, or however much time you have for an average move. And then you could say, how deep can I go? And you won't know for sure, because it depends on the game. So, in the earlier days of game-playing programs, the game-playing program left a lot of computation on the table, because it would make a decision in three seconds. And it might have made a much different move if it used all the competition it had available. Alternatively, it might be grinding away, and after two minutes was consumed. It had no move and just did something random.

Application

L05-3-1

A MAX root has three MIN children A, B and C. Their leaf scores are A:[4,9], B:[2,8], C:[6,7]. What scores back up, and which move should MAX choose?

  • A returns 4, B returns 2 and C returns 6.
  • MAX chooses C with value 6.

biggest number. There's another player, his opponent, which we'll call the minimizing player. And he's hoping that the play will go down to the board situation that's as small as possible. L05-3-2

A MAX root has already completed one MIN branch with value 5. In a second MIN branch, the first evaluated leaf is 3 and the other leaf is unknown. Can alpha-beta skip that unknown leaf, and why?

  • Yes: the second MIN branch can return at most 3 after seeing the first leaf.
  • The root already has 5, so this branch cannot improve its decision, regardless of the unknown leaf.

L05-3-3

For a uniform game tree with branching factor 4 and depth 6, how many leaf evaluations does plain minimax require? Using the lecture's ideal-order approximation 2*b^(d/2), what is the alpha-beta estimate?

  • Plain minimax evaluates 4^6 = 4096 leaves.
  • The ideal-order approximation gives 2*4^3 = 128; this is an ideal-case estimate, not a universal observed count.

Connections

L05-4-1

Compare the conditions for stopping a route search and stopping a depth-limited game search. How do branch-and-bound and minimax each decide that one candidate is better?

  • Branch-and-bound minimizes accumulated route cost and uses bounds to establish a shortest goal route.
  • Minimax uses backed-up board evaluations while accounting for an adversary; a good game move is not simply the shortest path to an attractive leaf.

L05-4-2

Both beam search and alpha-beta pruning discard branches. Why is it wrong to describe both as sacrificing answer quality merely to search faster?

  • Beam search imposes a width cutoff based on heuristic ranking and can discard the best solution.
  • Alpha-beta removes branches proven unable to change the minimax value for the same search horizon and evaluations.

L05-4-3

A minimax game tree and an identification tree are both trees. What do their internal nodes, branches and results represent, and why should their traversal rules differ?

  • A game tree branches on possible moves, alternates players and backs up adversarial values.
  • An identification tree branches on observed test outcomes and reaches a class label; it follows the input’s outcomes rather than optimizing against an opponent.
Transcript evidence

biggest number. There's another player, his opponent, which we'll call the minimizing player. And he's hoping that the play will go down to the board situation that's as small as possible. Because his view is the opposite of the maximizing player, hence the name minimax. But how does it work? Do you see which way the play is going to go? How do you decide which way the play is going to go? Well, it's not obvious at a glance. Do you see which way it's going to go? It's not obvious to the glance. It's very simple. You go down to the bottom of the tree, you compute static values, you back them up level by level, and then you decide where to go. And in this particular situation, the maximizer goes to the left. L06-1-3

In the robot-arm learning example, what motor outputs are recorded alongside joint positions, velocities and accelerations?

  • The torques on the two motors.
  • Those records associate observed motion states with the torques used to produce them.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 10: Introduction to Learning, Nearest Neighbors · 24:00–30:03 · 6:robot

And you don't have to strain yourself, much, to implement it. So that's cool. That's an example where we have a very non-standard metric. Now let's see, what else can we do? How about a robotic arm control? Here we go. We're going to just have a simple arm. And what we want to do is, we want to get this arm to move that ball along some trajectory at a speed, velocity, and acceleration that we have determined. So we've got two problems here. Well let's see, we've got two problems because, first of all, we've got angles, theta 1 and theta 2. It's a 2 degree of 3 of arm, so there are only two angles. So the first problem we have is the kinematic problem of translating the (x,y)-cordinates of the ball, the desired ones, into the theta 1, theta 2 space. That's simple kinematic problem. No f equals ma there. It Doesn't involve forces, or time, or acceleration, anything. Pretty simple. But then we've got the problem of getting it to go along that trajectory with positions, speeds, and accelerations that we desire. And now you say to me, well I've got 801, I can do that. And that's true, you can. Because, it's Newtonian mechanics. All you have to do is solve the equations. There are the equations. Good luck. Why are they so complicated? Well because of the complicated geometry. You notice we've got some products of theta 1 and theta 2 in there, somewhere, I think? You've got theta 2's. I see an acceleration squared. And yeah, there's a theta 1 dot times a theta 2 dot. A velocity times a velocity. Where the hell did that come from? I mean it's supposed to be f equals ma, right? Those are Coriolis forces, because of the complicated geometry. OK. So you hire Berthold Horn, or somebody, to work these equations out for you. And he comes up with something like this. And you try it out and it doesn't work. Why doesn't it work? It's Newtonian mechanics, I said. It doesn't work because we forgot to tell Berthold that there's friction in all the joints. And we forgot to tell him that they've worn a little bit since yesterday. And we forgot that the measurements we make on the lab table are not quite precise. So people try to do this. It just doesn't work. As soon as you get a ball of a different weight you have to start over. It's gross. So I don't know. I can do this sort of thing effortlessly, and I couldn't begin to solve those equations. So let's see. What we're going to do is we're going to forget about the problem for a minute. And we're going to talk about building ourselves a gigantic table. And here's what's going to be on the table. Theta 1, theta 2, theta 3, oops, there are only two. So that's theta 1 again, but it's the velocity, angular velocity. And then we have the accelerations. So we're going to have a big table of these things. And what we're going to do, is we're going to give this arm a childhood. And we're going to write down all the combinations we ever see, every 100 milliseconds, or something. And the arm is just going to wave around like a kid does in the cradle. And then, we're not quite done. Because there are two other things we're going to record. Can you guess what they are? There are going to be the torque on the first motor, and the torque on the second motor. And so now, we've got a whole bunch of those records. The question is, what do we got to do with it? Well here's what we're going to do it. We're going to divide this trajectory that we're hoping to achieve, up into little pieces. And there's a little piece. And in that little piece nothing is going to change much. There's going to be an acceleration, velocity, position. And so we can look those up in the table that we made in the childhood. And we'll look around and find the closest match, and this will be the set of values for the positions, velocities, and accelerations that are associated with that particular movement. And guess what we can do now? We can say, in the past, the torques associated with that particular little piece of movement lie right there. So we can just look it up. Now this method was thought up and rejected, because computers weren't powerful enough. And then, this is the age of recycling, right? So the idea got recycled when computers got strong enough. And it works pretty well, for things like this. But you might say to me, well can it do the stuff that we humans can do, like this? And the answer is, let's look.

Understanding

L06-2-1

Why does comparing raw word-count vectors by Euclidean distance risk retrieving the wrong articles for a short query, and how does the lecture address it?

  • The short query has small counts, so vector length can dominate distance even when subject matter differs.
  • Cosine/angle comparison emphasizes relative direction through a normalized dot product rather than raw magnitude.
Transcript evidence

L06-2-2

How does dividing each feature by its standard deviation address one nearest-neighbor problem, and what problem does it leave unresolved?

  • It puts feature spreads on comparable scales, giving each transformed feature unit variance.
  • It does not establish which features matter; irrelevant features can still distort the comparison.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 10: Introduction to Learning, Nearest Neighbors · 38:00–42:01 · 6:scaling

So that's the story on arm control. Now all this is pretty straightforward, easy to understand. And of course, there are some problems. Problem number one, what if the space of samples looks like this? [TAPPING ON CHALK BOARD] What's going to happen in that case? Well what's going to happen in that case is that the-- let's see, which values are going to be more important? The x values, right? The y values are spread out all over the place. So you'd like the spread of the data to sort of be the same in all the dimensions. So is there anything we can do to arrange for that to be true? Sure, we can just normalize the data. So we can borrow from our statistics course and say, well, let's see, we're interested in x. And we know that the variance of x is equal to 1 over n times the sum of the values, minus the mean value squared. That's a measure of how much the data spreads out. So now, instead of using x, we can use x prime, which is equal to x over sigma. What's the variance of that going to be? x over sigma sub x. Anybody see, instantaneously, what the variance of that's going be? Or do we have to work it out? It's going to be 1, Work out the algebra for me. It's obvious, it's simple. Just substitute x prime into this formula for variance, and do the algebraic high school manipulation. And you'll see that the variance turns out not to be of this new variable, this transformed variable you want. So that problem, the non uniformity problem, the spread problem, is easy to handle. What about that other problem? No cake without flour? What if it turns out that the data-- you have two dimensions and the answer, actually, doesn't depend on y at all. What will happen? Then you're often going to get screwy results, because it'll be measuring a distance that is merely confusing the answer. So problem number two is the what matters problem. Write it down, what matters. Problem number three is, what if the answer doesn't depend on the data at all? Then you've got the trying to build a cake without flour. Once somebody asked me-- a classmate of mine, who went on to become an important executive in an important credit card company-- asked me if we could use artificial intelligence to determine when somebody was going to go bankrupt?
L06-2-3

What does the nearest-neighbor lecture's cake-without-flour warning mean for a prediction task?

  • The supplied measurements may lack information about the target.
  • A learning procedure cannot reliably recover a relationship that the available features do not contain; feature relevance must be checked.
Transcript evidence

L06-3-1

A one-nearest-neighbor classifier uses Euclidean distance. Its only stored examples are A at (0,0) and B at (4,0). What label does query (3,1) receive? Show the two distances.

  • Distances are sqrt(10) to A and sqrt(2) to B.
  • The classifier chooses B.

For word-count vectors q=(1,1), A=(10,10) and B=(1,0), which article wins under Euclidean distance and which under cosine similarity? Explain the disagreement.

  • Euclidean distance selects B: distances are sqrt(162) to A and 1 to B.
  • Cosine selects A: similarities are 1 and 1/sqrt(2), respectively; A shares the query’s direction despite its larger magnitude.

So we'll divide by the magnitude of u, and we'll divide by the magnitude of the art vector to the article. So that's just the dot product right? That's a very fast computation. So with a very fast computation you can see if these things are going to be in the same direction. By the way, if this vector here is actually identical to one of those articles, what will the value be? Well then a cosine will be 0 and we'll get the maximum die of the cosine, which is 1. Yeah, that will do it. So if we use any of the articles to probe the article space, they'll find themselves, which is a good thing to have a mechanism do. OK. So that's just the dot product of those two vectors. And it works like a charm. It's not the most sophisticated way of doing these things. There are hairy ways. You can get a Ph.D. by doing this sort of stuff in some new and sophisticated way. But this is a simple way. It works pretty well.
L06-3-3

Two examples differ by 200 units on feature x and 2 units on feature y. Training-set standard deviations are sigma_x=100 and sigma_y=1. After the lecture's scaling, what are the differences and Euclidean distance? What changes about their relative contributions?

  • Scaled differences are 2 and 2; the distance is sqrt(8).
  • The dimensions now contribute equally to squared distance, whereas raw units let x dominate.

Connections

L06-4-1

Why is the identification-tree approach a useful alternative to the nearest-neighbor setup when inputs include symbolic tests and tests have different costs?

  • The numerical-distance setup needs a meaningful representation/metric; symbolic outcomes do not directly supply the same geometry.
  • A test tree can branch on symbolic outcomes and avoid unnecessary or expensive tests on particular paths.

Because of the cost. but there's another, more important reason why we want a small tree. Let me give you a hint. It's Occam's Razor. The simplest explanation is often the best explanation. So if you have a big, complicated explanation, that's probably less good than a simple, small explanation.
L06-4-2

Connect the robot-arm lookup example to Grimson's distinction between knowing a mathematical description and having a workable computational recipe. Why might recorded experience be useful even when equations of motion exist?

  • Equations describe the mechanics, but practical execution also depends on friction, wear and imperfect measurements.
  • Recording nearby motion states and motor torques supplies a procedure for choosing actions from experience rather than relying solely on an idealized analytic calculation.

Do not claim that all mathematical equations are declarative or that analytic control is impossible; the comparison concerns the particular example.

L06-4-3

Contrast using stored motion examples to control a robot arm with using an autocoder's small hidden layer to represent inputs. How is information reused in these two approaches?

  • The robot uses stored observations to find nearby motion conditions and associated torques.
  • The autocoder learns weights and a compressed hidden representation by reconstructing inputs; it does not simply look up a stored motion record.

L07-1-3

When the lecture converts an identification tree into rules, which part of the tree supplies one initial rule?

  • A path from the root to a leaf.
  • Tests/outcomes on that path become the conditions and the leaf label becomes the conclusion, initially giving one rule per leaf.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 11: Learning: Identification Trees, Disorder · 46:00–49:27 · 7:rules

What are the virtues of it? Number one, you don't use all the tests. You use only the test that seem to be doing some useful work for you. So that means that you do a better job, because your measurement technique is simpler. And it costs less, because you're not going to the expense of doing all of the testing. So it's a real winner. But you know what? Some classes of people-- not scientists, but I mean people like doctors and stuff. They don't like to look at these tress. They're kind of rule-oriented. So they look a tree like this for determining what kind of thyroid disease you have, and it would have maybe 20 or so tests in it of various kinds of hormones, like thyroxine and this and that. And they say, ah, we can't deal with that. So we have to work with them. So what we do is we convert the tree into a set of rules. How do we convert the tree into a set of rules? Oops, wrong one. Go away, go away. Here's what I want. Yeah, good. How would we convert this tree into a set of rules? It's straightforward. [INAUDIBLE], what do we do? STUDENT: You'd basically just look down each branch-- PATRICK WINSTON: You'd basically just go down each branch to a leaf. So you say, for example, here's one rule. If shadow equals question mark, and garlic equals oh, [INAUDIBLE] want to choose No. Doesn't eat garlic. No. I think I'll say Yes. Yes. That changes the answer. Then if it eats garlic, it's not a vampire, right? That's one of four possible rules, because there are four leaf nodes. Now, almost done. We are done, except for one thing. We can actually take these four rules, and start thinking about how to simplify them. You can ask questions like, if I have a rule that tests both the shadow and the garlic, do I actually need both of those antecedents? And the answer is, in many cases, no. And in particular, in this case, no. Because if we look at our data set, what we discover is that in the event that we're talking about a shadow question mark-- oh, I guess I had a better choice the other way. Oh, no. If you look at the garlic, all the garlics-- Yes, Yes, and Yes-- it turns out that the answer is no, independent of what the shadow condition is. So we can look at the rules, and in some cases, we'll discover that our tree is a little bit more complicated than it needs to be. We can actually get rid of some of the clauses. So in the end, we can develop a very simple mechanism based on good old fashioned rule-based behavior, like you saw almost in the beginning of the subject, that does the job. And now, without any royalty, you're all free to put this in your PDA and use it to protect yourself in the days to com, especially since Halloween's just around the corner.

Understanding

L07-2-1

Why might an identification tree use a measurement on one branch but never perform it on another?

  • A test can be useful only for a subset of cases after earlier outcomes are known.
  • Already homogeneous subsets need no further separation, so omitting tests can reduce cost.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 11: Learning: Identification Trees, Disorder · 03:01–09:06 · 7:tests

So what's different about this from the electrical cover data set? STUDENT: [INAUDIBLE] PATRICK WINSTON: Could you use the nearest neighbor technique to identify vampires with this data? This is symbolic. So we're not saying that your ability to cast a shadow is 0.7. You either cast a shadow, down cast a shadow, or we can't tell. It's a symbolic result. So problem number one we have to face with data of this kind is that it's not numeric. And there are other characteristics, as well. For example, it's not clear that all of these characteristics actually matter. So some characteristics don't matter. And a corollary to that is that some characteristics do matter, but they only matter part of the time. And finally, there's the matter of cost. Some of these tests may be more expensive to perform than others. For example, if you wanted to determine whether someone casts a shadow, you'd have to go to the trouble of getting up during daylight. That might be an expensive operation for you. You'd have to go find some garlic and ask them to eat it. That might be expensive. So some of these tests might be expensive relative to other tests. But once you realize that we are talking in terms of tests, and not a vector of real values, then what you do is clear. You build yourself a little tree of tests. So who knows how this problem will turn out? But you can imagine a situation where you have one test up here which might have three outcomes. And one but only one of those outcomes might require you to perform another test. And only when you've created the tree of tests that look like this are you finished. So given this set of tests and a set of samples, the question becomes, how do you arrange the tests in a tree like that so as to do the identification that you want to do? So since we're talking about identification, it's not surprising that this kind of tree is called an identification tree. And there's a tendency-- and I may slip into it myself-- to call this a decision tree. But a decision tree is a label for something else. This is an identification tree. And the task is to create a good one. So what is a good one versus a not so good one? And if they all have the same cost, then it's the number of tests. So overall, what you like is a small tree rather than a big one. So you might be able to take your sample data and divide it up, so that at the bottom of the tree, at the leaves, all of the sets that are produced by the tests are uniform, homogeneous. We'd like that tree to be the simplest possible tree you can find, not some big complicated one that also divides up all the data into uniform subsets. By uniform subset-- at the bottom of the tree, you have all of the vampires together, and all the non-vampires together. So you'd like a small tree. So why not just go all the way and do British Museum, and calculate all possible trees? Well, you can do that, but it's one of those NP problems. And as you know, NP problems suck in general. And so you don't want to do that. You want to have some kind of heuristic mechanism for building a small tree. And we want a small tree because-- Why do we want a small tree? Because of the cost. but there's another, more important reason why we want a small tree. Let me give you a hint. It's Occam's Razor. The simplest explanation is often the best explanation. So if you have a big, complicated explanation, that's probably less good than a simple, small explanation.

L07-2-2

Why does the lecture weight each branch's disorder by the fraction of samples entering that branch instead of adding all branch disorders equally?

  • An unweighted sum treats tiny and large branches as equally influential.
  • Weighting measures the remaining disorder across the sample population, taking branch sizes into account.
Transcript evidence

L07-2-3

For an identification tree using one numeric feature threshold at a time, why are boundaries axis-aligned, and why should a later split not automatically extend across the entire feature space?

  • A threshold on one coordinate creates a boundary parallel to the other coordinate axis.
  • A later test applies only to samples in its existing branch/region, not to regions already separated by earlier tests.
Transcript evidence

Application

L07-3-1

Using binary disorder H=-p*log2(p)-(1-p)*log2(1-p), compare a set with two positives and two negatives to a set with four positives and no negatives. What are the disorders and which is purer?

  • The balanced set has disorder 1 bit.
  • The pure set has disorder 0, using the limiting value 0*log2(0)=0; it is purer.
Transcript evidence

6.034 — Lecture 11: Learning: Identification Trees, Disorder · 24:00–29:58 · 7:entropy

disorder of a set from those information theory guys. So what we're going to do is exactly that. Let's put it over here, so we'll have it handy when we want to try to measure those things. The gospel according to information theorists is that the disorder, D, or some set is equal to-- now let's suppose that this is a set of binary values. So we have positives and then we have negatives. Pluses and minuses. But pluses, they don't go very well in an algebraic equation, because they might be confused with adding. So I'm going to say P and N. And then it'll be the total, which is P plus N. We only have two choices, positive and negative. So the disorder of set, according those guys, is equal to minus the number of positives over the total number, times the log to the base 2 of the positives over the total, minus the negatives over the total, times the log 2 of the negatives over the total. Those negatives look a little worrisome, because you think, well, maybe this thing can go negative. But that's not going to be true, right? Because these ratios are all less than 1, and the logarithm of something that's less than 1 is negative. So we're OK. So that's a lovely way of measuring disorder. L07-3-2

Test A splits eight examples into three branches: three positives, three negatives, and a balanced pair. Test B splits the same examples into two balanced groups of four. Calculate weighted disorder for each and choose the preferred split by the lecture's criterion.

  • A has (3/8)*0 + (3/8)*0 + (2/8)*1 = 0.25.
  • B has (4/8)*1 + (4/8)*1 = 1, so A is preferred because its remaining weighted disorder is lower.

L07-3-3

Training examples on a single numeric feature are 1:N, 2:N, 4:P, 5:P. Following the lecture's candidate-threshold method, list the three midpoint thresholds and select the one producing pure children.

  • The midpoint candidates are 1.5, 3 and 4.5.
  • Threshold 3 separates both N examples from both P examples and gives zero disorder in each child.

Connections

L07-4-1

Connect the Python lecture's distinction between kinds of values with the identification-tree lecture's difficulty applying its earlier numeric nearest-neighbor setup to vampire tests. What representation issue must be solved?

  • Values have types and meanings that constrain useful operations; a symbolic outcome is not automatically a meaningful numeric distance.
  • Tests such as shadow=yes/no/unknown can be handled directly by branches; arbitrary numeric codes would need an appropriate metric before distance-based comparison makes sense.
Transcript evidence

So who knows how this problem will turn out? But you can imagine a situation where you have one test up here which might have three outcomes. And one but only one of those outcomes might require you to perform another test. And only when you've created the tree of tests that look like this are you finished. So given this set of tests and a set of samples, the question becomes, how do you arrange the tests in a tree like that so as to do the identification that you want to do? So since we're talking about identification, it's not surprising that this kind of tree is called an identification tree. And there's a tendency-- and I may slip into it myself-- to call this a decision tree. But a decision tree is a label for something else. This is an identification tree. And the task is to create a good one. So what is a good one versus a not so good one? What characteristic would you like for a decision tree-- for an identification trade to have, if you're going to call it good identification tree? What do you think, Krishna? And if they all have the same cost, then it's the number of tests. So overall, what you like is a small tree rather than a big one. So you might be able to take your sample data and divide it up, so that at the bottom of the tree, at the leaves, all of the sets that are produced by the tests are uniform, homogeneous. We'd like that tree to be the simplest possible tree you can find, not some big complicated one that also divides up all the data into uniform subsets. By uniform subset-- at the bottom of the tree, you have all of the vampires together, and all the non-vampires together. So you'd like a small tree. So why not just go all the way and do British Museum, and calculate all possible trees? Well, you can do that, but it's one of those NP problems. And as you know, NP problems suck in general. And so you don't want to do that. You want to have some kind of heuristic mechanism for building a small tree. And we want a small tree because-- Why do we want a small tree? Because of the cost. but there's another, more important reason why we want a small tree. Let me give you a hint. It's Occam's Razor. The simplest explanation is often the best explanation. So if you have a big, complicated explanation, that's probably less good than a simple, small explanation.
L07-4-2

The identification-tree lecture favors a small tree, but the deep-net demonstration trains a wide network before reducing it. Why are these positions not necessarily contradictory?

  • A small final representation can reduce complexity or cost.
  • Finding that representation is a separate training problem: the wider net can reach a solution that a small network trained from scratch fails to find in the demonstration.

The neural-net observation is about the demonstrated optimization behavior, not a theorem that wider is always better.

Transcript evidence

What characteristic would you like for a decision tree-- for an identification trade to have, if you're going to call it good identification tree? What do you think, Krishna? What would be a good characteristic? STUDENT: Maybe the minimum number of levels? PATRICK WINSTON: Yeah. He said minimum number of levels. What's another way you could say what a good one is? Each test costs something, right? So what's another way of thinking about what a good tree would look like? STUDENT: Minimum cost. PATRICK WINSTON: The minimum cost. And if they all have the same cost, then it's the number of tests. So overall, what you like is a small tree rather than a big one. So you might be able to take your sample data and divide it up, so that at the bottom of the tree, at the leaves, all of the sets that are produced by the tests are uniform, homogeneous. We'd like that tree to be the simplest possible tree you can find, not some big complicated one that also divides up all the data into uniform subsets. By uniform subset-- at the bottom of the tree, you have all of the vampires together, and all the non-vampires together. So you'd like a small tree. So why not just go all the way and do British Museum, and calculate all possible trees? Well, you can do that, but it's one of those NP problems. And as you know, NP problems suck in general. And so you don't want to do that. You want to have some kind of heuristic mechanism for building a small tree. And we want a small tree because-- Why do we want a small tree? Because of the cost. but there's another, more important reason why we want a small tree. Let me give you a hint. It's Occam's Razor. The simplest explanation is often the best explanation. So if you have a big, complicated explanation, that's probably less good than a simple, small explanation.

L07-4-3

Why can if-then rules obtained from an identification tree be useful for classification even though the games lecture says simple board-to-move rules are not enough for strong chess play?

  • Identification rules map observed test outcomes to a class using a learned partition.
  • Choosing a chess move requires accounting for future consequences and an opponent; a current-board rule can omit those effects.
Transcript evidence

biggest number. There's another player, his opponent, which we'll call the minimizing player. And he's hoping that the play will go down to the board situation that's as small as possible. Because his view is the opposite of the maximizing player, hence the name minimax. But how does it work? Do you see which way the play is going to go? How do you decide which way the play is going to go? Well, it's not obvious at a glance. Do you see which way it's going to go? It's not obvious to the glance. And you see how we can just keep doing this? Now the maximizing player can see that if he goes to the left, he gets a score of 2. If he goes to the right, he only gets a score of 1. So, he's going to go to the left. So, overall, then, the maximizing player is going to have a 2 as the perceived value of that situation there at the top. That's the minimax algorithm. It's very simple. You go down to the bottom of the tree, you compute static values, you back them up level by level, and then you decide where to go. And in this particular situation, the maximizer goes to the left. And the minimizer goes to the left, too, so the play ends up here, far short of the 8 that the maximizer wanted and less than the 1 that the minimizer wanted. But this is an adversarial game. You're competing with each other. So, you don't expect to get what you want, right? So, maybe we ought to see if we can make that work. There's a game tree. Do you see how it goes? L08-1-1

In the neural-net lecture, how is a neuron's threshold represented so it can be treated as another weight?

  • Add a constant input of -1.
  • Give that input weight w0=t, subtracting t from the summed input and shifting the decision threshold to zero.
Transcript evidence

So maybe what we ought to do is if this guy is much bigger than this guy, it would suggest we mostly want to move in this direction, or to put it in 1801 terms, what we're going to do is we're going to follow the gradient. And so the change in the w vector is going to equal to this partial derivative times i plus this partial derivative times j. The first part of the answer is those thresholds are annoying. They're just extra baggage to deal with. What we really like instead of c being a function of xw and t was we'd like c prime to be a function f prime of x and the weights. But we've got to account for the threshold somehow. So here's how you do that. What you do is you say let us add another input to this neuron. And it's going to have a weight w0. And it's going to be connected to an input that's always minus 1. You with me so far? Now what we're going to do is we're going to say, let w0 equal t. What does that do to the movement of the threshold? What it does is it takes that threshold and moves it back to 0. So this little trick here takes this pink threshold and redoes it so that the new threshold box looks like this. Think about it. If this is t, and this is minus 1, then this is minus t. And so this thing ought to fire if everything's over-- if the sum is over 0. So it makes sense. And it gets rid of the threshold thing for us. So now we can just think about weights. But still, we've got that step function there. And that's not good. So what we're going to do is we're going to smooth that guy out. So this is trick number two. Instead of a step function, we're going to have this thing we lovingly call a sigmoid function, because it's kind of from an s-type shape. And the function we're going to use is this one-- one, well, better make it a little bit different-- 1 over 1 plus e to the minus whatever the input is. Let's call the input alpha. Does that makes sense? Is alpha is 0, then it's 1 over 1 plus 1 plus one half. If alpha is extremely big, then even the minus alpha is extremely small. And it becomes one. It goes up to an asymptotic value of one here. On the other hand, if alpha is extremely negative, than the minus alpha is extremely positive. And it goes to 0 asymptotically. So we got the right look to that function. It's a very convenient function.
L08-1-2

Which smooth activation replaces the hard step in the neural-net lecture, and what happens to its output at very large positive and negative inputs?

  • The logistic sigmoid, 1/(1+exp(-a)).
  • It approaches 1 for very large positive a and 0 for very large negative a.

L08-1-3

In the small-network training demonstration, what happens when the rate constant is raised from 0.5 to 5 and then to 50?

  • The increase to 5 produces much faster convergence in the shown trials.
  • At 50 the trials worsen and show instability; increasing the step size is not always beneficial.

L08-2-1

Why does Winston put a minus sign on the squared-error performance function, and does this change what counts as a perfect prediction?

  • He wants improvement to mean going uphill/maximizing rather than minimizing.
  • Zero error remains the optimum; maximizing negative squared error and minimizing squared error seek the same fit.
Transcript evidence

L08-2-2

Why is a hard step activation inconvenient for the gradient-based training procedure introduced in the lecture?

  • Its discontinuity and flat regions do not provide the smooth, useful derivatives needed for the proposed updates.
  • A sigmoid supplies a differentiable transition that allows weight effects to be propagated through the network.
Transcript evidence

L08-2-3

Why does backpropagation not need to separately recompute every possible path through a multilayer network?

  • Chain-rule expressions share downstream partial results.
  • Reusing those results across connections avoids the apparent exponential path enumeration; work follows the connections and layers instead.
Transcript evidence

So it isn't going to explode exponentially, because the influence-- let me say it one more time. The influences of changes of changes in p on the performance is all we care about when we come back to this part of the network, because this stuff cannot influence the performance except by going through this column of p's. So it's not going to blow up exponentially. We're going to be able to reuse a lot of the computation. So it's the reuse principle. Have we ever seen the reuse principle at work before. Not exactly. But you remember that little business about the extended list? We know that we've seen-- we know we've seen something before. So we can stop computing. It's like that. We're going to be able to reuse the computation. We've already done it to prevent an exponential blowup. By the way, for those of you who know about fast Fourier transform-- same kind of idea-- reuse of partial results. So in the end, what can we say about this stuff? In the end, what we can say is that it's linear in depth. That is to say if we increase the number of layers to so-called depth, then we're going to increase the amount of computation necessary in a linear way, because the computation we need in any column is going to be fixed. What about how it goes with respect to the width? Well, with respect to the width, any neuron here can be connected to any neuron in the next row. So the amount of work we're going to have to do will be proportional to the number of connections. So with respect to width, it's going to be w-squared. But the fact is that in the end, this stuff is readily computed. And this, phenomenally enough, was overlooked for 25 years. So what is it in the end?

For the lecture's sigmoid s(a)=1/(1+exp(-a)), what are s(0) and its derivative at a=0? Use the derivative form expressed in terms of the output.

  • s(0)=1/2.
  • s prime = s*(1-s), so s prime at 0 is 1/4.

L08-3-2

A weight is w=2, the derivative of the performance to be maximized is dP/dw=-0.4, and the rate constant is 0.1. Using gradient ascent, what is the updated weight? Does one such step prove a global optimum has been found?

  • w_new = 2 + 0.1*(-0.4) = 1.96.
  • No; a local gradient update does not establish global optimality.

L08-3-3

A small network has a single dependency chain w -> h -> z -> P. At the current point, dh/dw=2, dz/dh=0.3 and dP/dz=-4. What is dP/dw, and which local derivatives are combined?

  • Multiply along the chain: (-4)*0.3*2 = -2.4.
  • The result combines the performance-to-output, output-to-hidden, and hidden-to-weight derivatives.
